14:["$","p",null,{"children":["This sheet (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#1-hiding-failures","children":"Section 1"}],") works through one concrete example from ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#bib-lang2024porlhf","children":"Lang et al. 2024"}]," to see how naive RLHF can fail when the human evaluator only ",["$","em",null,{"children":"partially"}]," observes the environment they are providing feedback on. By the end of the sheet you will have derived, by hand, the conditions under which an RLHF-optimal policy hides information from the human in a way that systematically inflates the human's perception of the policy's return — the failure mode the paper calls ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"deceptive inflation"}],". We close with an informal discussion of the dual failure mode, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"overjustification"}],", in which the agent pays real reward to make its (already-good) behavior look as good as it is, and briefly discuss how such failure modes motivate approaches like AI safety via debate."]}]

The remaining problems made this concrete: when ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"p > 1/3","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"<span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"base\"><span class=\"strut\" style=\"height:0.7335em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">&gt;</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"base\"><span class=\"strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1/3</span></span></span>"}}]," and ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"p_{H} < 5/(5+r)","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"$d7"}}],", the hiding policy ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"[a_{I} a_{H} a_{T}]","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"$d8"}}]," and the honest policy ","$Ld9"," produce indistinguishable observations on failure, and RLHF picks the hider. This is ","$Lda",": the agent exploits the human's uncertainty to inflate the perceived return (","$Ldb",", Section 4)."]}]
dc:T5a0,<span class="katex-html" aria-hidden="true"><span class="base"><span class="strut" style="height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;"></span><span class="mopen">[</span><span class="mord"><span opposite regime. When ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"p","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"<span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"base\"><span class=\"strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span>"}}]," is small (CUDA isn't worth attempting) and ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"p_{H}","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"<span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"base\"><span class=\"strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.3283em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:0em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>"}}]," is large (the human strongly suspects every empty log of hiding a failure), the honest policy ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"[a_{I} a_{T}]","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"$dc"}}]," produces an empty post-driver log that ",["$","em",null,{"children":"looks"}]," like a hidden failure to the suspicious human. RLHF then prefers the wasteful ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","role":"math","aria-label":"[a_{I} a_{C} a_{T}]","dangerouslySetInnerHTML":{"__html":"$dd"}}],", which attempts CUDA only to produce an unambiguous log that produces an open failure. The agent pays real reward to prove its honesty: this is ","$Lde"," (","$Ldf",", Section 4)."]}]
3f:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The bigger picture."}],"  The CUDA example is small, but the phenomenon is general: any alignment-by-feedback method (RLHF, RLAIF, constitutional methods, ...) grades the agent by ",["$","em",null,{"children":"whatever the evaluator can tell"}],", not by what is true. Partial observability is one cause of that gap, but limits on expertise, attention, or time produce the same trap. This is the motivation for proposals like AI safety via debate (which we discussed yesterday): two AIs argue in front of a human judge, each pointing out flaws in the other, in the hope that the judge reaches a correct conclusion they could not reach unaided. Whether debate actually escapes the trap remains open; the point is that any feedback-based method has to confront the gap between \"what the human can tell\" and \"what is true.\""]}]
